The survey queried more than 8,000 respondents across eight countries: Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Poland, Spain,
the United Kingdom and the United States. It was conducted as a web-based survey between 22 April and 25 May 2026.

## Document 291: Food For Thought_ Sowing The Seeds of Food Inflation (2026-07-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Food For Thought_ Sowing The Seeds of Food Inflation (2026-07-15).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-07-15
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Food For Thought: Sowing 
The Seeds of Food Inflation

1 
Authors 
Maui Brennan 
Economist

Sanjay Raja 
Chief UK Economist

Maria Contreras 
Economist

July 15, 2026 
Ongoing tensions in the Middle East have raised the prospect of indirect effects 
on inflation - particularly in relation to food prices. Indeed, much has been said 
about the rise in energy prices and its spill over into other commodities such as 
fertiliser, CO2, and other key ingredients that sit within the agricultural pipeline. 
To be sure, both the size and duration of the shock matter for food prices. 
In this note, we do four things. 
— First, we highlight what’s happened to key commodity prices in the wake of 
the Iran shock. 
— Second, we set out a framework, estimating the potential impact of 
commodity price moves on food inflation for both the euro area and the UK. 
— Third, we stress test our estimates by looking at the duration of the shock 
and whether pass-through of cost increases change when the shock is 
deemed 'transitory' or 'persistent' . 
— Finally, we highlight some of the broader implications for inflation and where 
risks lie around our estimates. 
Our analysis points to renewed upside risks to food prices stemming from recent 
oil and fertiliser market developments. The effects appear larger in the UK than in 
the Euro area. 
Importantly, we find that pass-through isn't immediate, and in fact, can build 
over a long period of time given the mapping of commodity prices into food retail 
prices. What also matters is whether shocks prove persistent or transitory. 
Notwithstanding the MoU agreed to by the US and Iran, we estimate that the net 
effects from oil and urea prices will lead to modest inflationary pressure in the 
quarters ahead – something we will be watching for closely. That said, 
considerable uncertainty surrounds both the scale and timing of these effects, 
with a range of other supply-side factors also likely to influence outcomes. We 
will be monitoring market developments closely and will calibrate our 
projections accordingly.

### Page 2

Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
A recap on recent price movements
Recent geopolitical developments have been associated with large swings in
fertiliser and oil prices. Oil prices initially rose as markets priced supply-disruption
concerns. Subsequently, however, price rises retraced meaningfully as these
concerns eased with continuing peace deal negotiations. To add context to these
movements, we compare spot and futures prices before the episode, after the initial

Iran shock, and using the latest available market data. For the futures curves, we fit
a Nielsen-Siegel curve to trace out the shape of the curve across contract positions.
For fertiliser prices, we use the World Bank urea price series as a proxy for global
nitrogen fertiliser costs. Urea is one of the most widely traded nitrogen fertilisers
and is closely linked to the production process for agricultural inputs. We note,
however, that this captures only one important fertiliser-cost channel rather than

the complete range of farm input costs.
Figure 1: Brent futures pricing downward sloping...
Source : Deutsche Bank, Bloomberg Finance LP
Figure 2: …same as urea futures pricing
Source : Deutsche Bank, Bloomberg Finance LP
Before the Iran War, the spot price for Brent crude oil was trading at about USD
72.50/barrel. This then spiked to USD 118/barrel during the peak of the Iran/US
conflict, before settling at around USD 83/barrel at current market prices (figure 1).

Interestingly, the futures curve is currently flatter than the steeper curve observed
at the start of the war, which could imply a more gradual decline in prices in the next
few months. The urea spot price also increased sharply at the beginning of the Iran
war. In contrast, these prices have settled at a much lower level (figure 2).
Perhaps unsurprisingly, in terms of recent food price developments, we have yet to
see any tangible impact on retail prices.

Euro area food inflation has softened more than expected this year, declining from
2.5% y-o-y in Dec-25 to 1.6% in Jun-26 based on the flash estimate. This marks the
lowest reading for food HICP since mid-2021, with the bulk of the adjustment
occurring over the past two months. Focusing on food stuff, the largest
disinflationary impulses have come from dairy products, sugar and sweets, and
coffee. In 2025, these categories were among the main drivers of food inflation,

accounting for roughly 35% of annual food inflation despite representing only 18%
of the HICP food basket. In recent months, their contribution has fallen to just over
20%. Meat prices have also eased somewhat from their 2025 peak, although they
remain the largest contributor to food inflation.
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 3

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
In the UK, we see a similar story. Heavy discounting has led to a drop-off in food
prices. Food and non-alcoholic beverages CPI slowed to 2.2% y-o-y – reaching its
lowest rate in 17 months. The slowdown in food prices can be seen across both
processed and unprocessed food. The latest PPI data for food points to further
disinflation in the pipeline, with producer prices across food, beverages and
tobacco slowing to 1.1% y-o-y in May-26.

Figure 3: Food (and non-alcoholic beverages) inflation in the EA and the UK has
been on a downtrend this year
Source : Deutsche Bank, Macrobond
How does energy and fertiliser costs pass-through into food inflation? There are
two channels in which oil and fertiliser prices work their way into CPI.
Channel 1: the direct energy cost. Oil enters food at pretty much every node in the
food chain. For example, manufacturers need diesel for tractors and irrigation

pumps, marine freight and trucking, processing heat, packaging (plastics) and
refrigeration. Energy-related inputs account for around 5–10% of farm production
costs, although the share can be significantly higher in energy-intensive sectors
such as horticulture and poultry.
Channel 2: fertiliser cost. Urea is essentially manufactured from natural gas. The
higher gas prices go, the higher fertiliser prices go too. The response can effectively
be summarised into two segments: an immediate margin squeeze, then a supply

response as farmers cut application rates or switch acreage to lower Nitrogen crops
(soybeans over maize), which lifts prices with a lag of a full crop cycle.
The transmission from input costs to retail prices, as a result, is heavily staggered.
The upstream leg is fast: ammonia and urea reprice off gas within weeks. Put
simply, an energy shock is visible in fertiliser markets almost immediately. The
middle leg is slow and seasonal — application decisions are made pre-season, so

a nitrogen cut driven by a spring price spike doesn't show up until (as early) as the
following harvest. Ultimately, the final leg is both lagged and heavily damped.
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 4

Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Estimating pass-through to food prices
There are several ways to estimate the effect of oil and fertilizer price shocks to food
inflation. In this case, we opt for a simple approach that allows the "data to speak":
the local projection method (Jorda, 2005). Essentially what we do is trace out the
impact of a shock over time, in this case oil and fertilizer price shocks, by running
a separate regression for each forecast horizon (1-month ahead, 2-months ahead

and so on...).
A key challenge is identifying the commodity-price shock itself. Oil and fertiliser
prices move for many different reasons, including global demand, exchange rates,
trade conditions, inflationary pressures and supply disruptions. To reduce the risk
of conflating commodity-specific surprises with broader macroeconomic
developments, we use a Romer and Romer-style approach (Romer & Romer, 2004).
The above approach involves regressing the shocks of interest on a set of potentially

confounding variables, including the USD effective exchange rate, monthly dummy
variables to capture seasonality, lags of the shocks of interest to account for
persistence, global trade volumes and global CPI inflation. We then extract the
residuals from this regression. These residuals could then be interpreted as
movements in oil and urea prices that are not explained by our macroeconomic
controls.
The shocks of interest that we use are the month-on-month percentage changes in

Brent crude oil spot prices and the World Bank urea commodity price series.
For the local projections, we use the HICP Food & Non-alcoholic beverages series
for the Euro area and the UK Consumer Price Index Food and Non-alcoholic
beverages. The regressions include the shock of interest (oil & urea), and three lags
of the price variables. We then estimate the cumulative percent impact on the price
level from a percentage point surprise in both oil and urea costs.

Baseline results
The baseline estimates suggest that oil and fertiliser price surprises have a
measurable effect on food prices. The estimated response varies across the two
economies and commodities, with the UK showing larger pass-through than the
euro area in our specifications. Our estimates, however, become relatively more
uncertain the further out we go as shown by the widening error bands.
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 5

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Figure 4: A 1pp shock in oil price leads to nearly a 7bps
increase in food HICP (EA)...
Source :Deutsche Bank
Figure 5:...and just over 10bps in the UK (CPI)
Source :Deutsche Bank
Figure 6: A 1pp shock to urea prices results in just over
4bps to EA HICP (food & non-alcoholic beverages)...
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 7:...and close to 6bps for UK CPI (food and non-
alcoholic beverages)
Source : Deutsche Bank
In further detail, figures 4-7 present the impact of a 1 percentage point increase in

monthly oil and urea price growth on the cumulative food & non-alcoholic
beverages price level. Looking first at fertiliser price shocks, we find that the peak
impact on UK food prices occurs after around 20 months with a 0.07% cumulative
impact on the food price level (figure 7). In the Euro area, the peak impact is slightly
lower at around 0.05% and also occurs after roughly 20 months (figure 6). A broadly
similar pattern is visible for oil price shocks. The UK experiences a larger cumulative

increase of 0.1% in food prices while the Euro area sees an impact of about 0.07%.
There are a few possible explanations for the slightly larger estimated effects in the
UK relative to the Euro area. One possible explanation that may be driving the
difference in pass-through between the Euro area and the UK is the higher imported
share in the UK food basket. This would make importers and producers more
exposed to externally driven input cost pressures.

A second explanation may come from sterling weakness during global energy
shocks.
A third explanation may be in fact that many UK farming sectors operate on narrow
margins. When energy, fertiliser or feed costs rise sharply, farmers have less ability
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 6

Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
to absorb the shock and are more likely to pass it on through the supply chain.
Defra's Farm Business Survey consistently shows that profitability is highly
sensitive to movements in input costs. UK agriculture is also relatively intensive,
particularly in cereals, oilseeds and grassland systems.
The impact of the Iran war
So far, the results have shown a 1 percentage point increase in fertiliser and oil price
inflation. However, events such as the Iran war have led to big swings in both urea

and oil prices – and in both directions. In order to better gauge these impacts, we
scale the shocks in the baseline results to the actual movements observed from
March to June. The figures below show the estimated cumulative food price pass-
through implied by each monthly commodity price move.
Figure 8: Estimated urea shock pass-through for each
month since the conflict began in the EA...
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 9:...and the UK
Source : Deutsche Bank

Figure 10: Scaled oil shock between March and June for
the EA...
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 11: ...and the UK
Source : Deutsche Bank
To be sure, the March shock impact alone would have had a big impact on food
prices in the Euro area and the UK. For both oil and urea, it was an upside surprise
of over 40 percentage points (over 3 standard deviations). However, over the course
of the past few months, there have been significant reversals in both oil and urea

prices. We attempt to capture these offsetting shocks and obtain the overall impact
since March. How do we do this? We do this by aggregating the impacts of the
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 7

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
monthly urea and oil price shocks – i.e. we aggregate the shocks from oil and urea
via superposition of the estimated local projections.
Figure 12: Estimated aggregate urea shock on food HICP
in the EA...
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 13:...and on UK food CPI
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 14: Estimated aggregated oil shock on food HICP in
the EA…
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 15:...and on UK food CPI
Source : Deutsche Bank

We trace out these shocks and find some interesting implications for the overall
impact. For the UK, we see a cumulative impact of around 1% on food prices from
the oil shocks a year from March and 0.3% from a urea shock peaking also in a year's
time from March, i.e. totalling nearly 130bps (though the effect starts to unwind
four quarters out). For the Euro area, the oil shocks lead to about a 0.5% cumulative
increase in a year from March and about a 0.3% impact from urea shocks in a year

from March. Overall, we observe similar patterns where the UK experiences a
slightly stronger impact from the recent shocks compared to the Euro area.
Stress testing our results: persistent vs transitory shocks
A simple scaling of the shocks is unlikely to fully reflect firms’ pricing behaviour. In
practice, firms may respond differently to the size and duration of the shock. A large
but temporary increase in oil or fertiliser prices may generate less pass-through

than a smaller but more sustained rise.
To capture this distinction, we split our oil and fertiliser shocks between persistent
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 8

Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
and transitory. We classify a shock as persistent if the price remains at least half as
large after four months. If the price falls by more than half over that period, we treat
it as transitory. We only apply this classification to sizeable shocks, defined as
moves larger than one standard deviation in the relevant shock series. This gives us
a simple way to separate temporary price spikes from more sustained input-cost
pressures.

We then run local projections again on the persistent and short-lived shocks and we
obtain the following results:
Figure 16: Persistent urea price shock...
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 17:...vs transitory urea price shock
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 18: Persistent oil price shock...
Source : Deutsche Bank
Figure 19: ...vs transitory oil price shock
Source : Deutsche Bank
The results suggest that persistence matters, particularly for oil shocks (figures 18

and 19). When the shock is persistent, we find a stronger peak impact for both the
Euro area and the United Kingdom  for a given 1 percentage point surprise
compared to a short-lived shock. For urea, the pattern is clearer in the UK, where
persistent shocks are associated with a larger food price pass-through. In the Euro
area, however, the difference between persistent and short-lived shocks is less
clear. Overall, the evidence points to a stronger role for shock persistence in the UK,

while the Euro area results are more mixed.
While it is too early to definitely classify the latest energy price shocks, the above
results highlight an important distinction. If recent recent rises in oil and fertiliser
prices prove short-lived and unwind over the coming months, then pass-through to
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 9

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
food prices may be more limited. However, if prices remain elevated, history
suggests we could expect a larger and more sustained impact on food inflation.
Based on the shocks observed so far, our classification leans towards the recent
developments being transitory in nature. That said, the classification is far from
certain and will depend on how oil and fertiliser prices evolve through July and the
months ahead.
Conclusions

What to make from the above analyses? We highlight several key findings.
First, the impact of a fertiliser and oil price shock and its pass-through to food price
levels tends to be stronger in the United Kingdom than in the Euro area.
Second, after scaling and aggregating the fertiliser and oil price shocks from March
up to June, we find that in a year's time from March, the cumulative impact on the
UK food price level could be about 1.3% while for the Euro area it would be around

0.8% instead – implying a 0.1-0.15pp increase in headline HICP/CPI.
Third, we also recognise the limitations of simply scaling these shocks. The size and
duration of the shock can result in very different implications for food price pass-
through. The persistence of a shock can have a much larger impact, especially in the
UK. It's too early to say where the current energy shock will fall into. Recent price
action suggests a further wave of energy pressures on the back of escalating Middle

East tensions. We will be monitoring this closely. The longer commodity prices
remain elevated, the more they feed through into food prices.
Lastly, the confidence bands around our estimates suggest wide variation in pass-
through. Given the swings in price rises across oil and urea, the staggered pass-
through of cost rises into the food supply chain may be more varied. While our
estimates provide a measure of the average conditional pass-through, other factors

will also impact pass-through. For example, warmer global temperatures, less
rainfall, and changing labour costs alongside ongoing geopolitical tensions may
also shape pricing strategies.
To be sure, despite the pull back in oil and urea prices from their peak, the net effects
still point to a modest jump in food (and non-alcoholic beverages) prices.
Furthermore, the recent jump back in price momentum will do little to assuage
concerns of indirect effects brewing within inflation. We will reflect some of our

findings over the coming week(s) as we update our inflation projections.
15 July 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 10

Deutsche Bank Research Institute

Editorial 
Geopolitics, policy and earnings 
Strategy summary:  The Houthis’ blockade of the Red Sea 
following the resumption of US -Iran hostilities has driven a 
sharp rebound in energy prices and bond yields. We expect US 
electoral constraints and Iranian restraint to eventually limit 
escalation in the conflict. Agai nst this backdrop, central banks 
are likely to hold rates this month as they assess the impact of 
any temporary spike in energy prices on the inflation outlook.  
Besides the spike in bond yields, equity markets will have to 
contend with lofty earnings expectations as the reporting 
season picks up. We expect strong AI investment -driven 
earnings beats, especially in the US and Asia, to drive upgrades 
to profit estimates. This should support continued equity market 
outperformance in the coming months. In fixed income, 
inflation-protected bonds appear particularly attractive, 
especially in the US, where the inflation -adjusted yield has 
surged above 2.4%, close to the highest since 2008.  
Mideast conflict limited by US electoral constraints, Iran 
restraint: The re-escalation of tensions, with Yemen’s Houthis 
joining the conflict, has driven European gas prices back to 
March peaks, but US gasoline prices remain well below the 
latest peaks. Notably, Iran has avoided attacking Saudi Arabia 
and UAE in the latest  escalation, signalling restraint. While 
there is a window for further near-term escalation, any rebound 
in US gasoline prices closer to May’s peaks in the run -up to 
November’s mid -term elections is likely to put pressure on 
President Trump to resume negot iations. Thus, we expect US 
crude oil (WTI) to trade largely in the USD 70 -90/bbl range in 
the next few months, limiting the economic impact.    
Central banks to hold rates this month.  We expect major 
central banks to hold rates this month as they assess the 
inflationary impact of the latest Middle East escalation. This was 
apparent from this week’s ECB decision to hold its deposit rate 
at 2.25%, while it kept the option open for another rate hike later 
this year. Meanwhile, the softer -than-expected US inflation 
report for June confirmed our view that inflation peaked in Q2. 
We expect the Fed to stay on hold this year as long as gasoline 
prices remain subdued, given slowing wage growth and cooling 
shelter and services inflation. As such, the 2-5-year segment of 
the bond yield curve looks increasingly attractive, especially if 
rate expectations peak with oil prices in the next few weeks.  
ECB, BoJ under rising pressure to hike. The ECB, being an 
inflation-targeting central bank, is under growing pressure to 
hike rates in the coming months to counter any energy -price 
driven inflation if European natural gas prices stay elevated. We 
raise the probability of another 25bps rate hike later this year, 
with the timing dependent on the status of the US -Iran conflict 
and the pass-through of energy prices onto inflation and wage 
growth. The BoE is likely to be constrained from hiking rates, 
though, due to weakening domestic growth outlook. Meanwhile, 
the BoJ remains behind the curve as a weaker currency pours 
fuel on domestic wage -driven inflation. There is rising chance 
the BoJ hikes twice this year, if only to support the JPY. 
Equities under rates, earnings scanner. Equities slumped on 
Thursday weighed by a Big Tech earnings report, surging crude 
oil prices (WTI crossed USD 90/bbl and Brent rose above USD 
100/bbl) and the US 10 -year government bond yield rising 
above 4.7%. The 10-year yield has gained almost 35bps since 
29 June. We believe a 25bps rise in bond yields, which serves 
as the discount rate for valuing equities, can impact global 
equities by around 3-4%, so investors should remain disciplined 
as rate expectations shift. However, projected earnings growth 
of 15 -20% in 2026 an d 2027 should provide a meaningful 
cushion against rate-driven valuation pressure. 
US earnings key support for equity outperformance . Big 
Tech is likely to show strong results and clearer AI monetisation 
in Q2. The tech sector has driven over half of global equity EPS 
upgrades since end-February, while AI adoption is still early in 
a multi-year cycle. We remain overweight US technology and 
communication services, and favour regions with stronger 
earnings visibility, particularly the US and Asia ex-Japan.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Top client questions 
  What do you expect from US Big Tech earnings next week? 
Our view: We expect US Big Tech companies to deliver solid Q2 
earnings while demonstrating further evidence  of AI 
monetisation. We are Overweight the US technology and 
communication services sectors. 
Rationale: The US technology sector continues to be a primary 
driver of S&P500 earnings, with the highest projected earnings 
growth over the next 12 months. It is expected to see earnings 
growth of 65.6% y/y in Q2 and 57% in 2026, driven by AI demand. 
Besides strong earnings growth, we expect US Big Tech companies 
to demonstrate that elevated AI capex is translating into stronger 
monetisation. US Census Bureau data indicates that AI adoption 
has already reached 20 -25% across US industries. This rapid 
uptake signals strong enterprise acceptance and gives us greater 
confidence that AI usage remains in the early stages of a multi-year 
growth cycle. The emergence of cost-effective and high-performing 
Chinese AI models has raised some concerns about AI 
commoditisation, which  can reduce the returns on AI capex. 
However, we believe that as AI resources become more efficient to 
use, total consumption will rise (Jevons paradox). To gain exposure, 
we are Overweight the US technology and communication services 
sectors, with the latter being dominated by large internet platforms. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist 
 What is your Q2 earnings outlook for the Japan and Europe 
financial sectors? 
Our view: We expect a constructive earnings season for Japan 
and Europe ex-UK banks and remain Overweight both sectors. 
Rationale: The earnings backdrop for Japan banks is supported by 
the BoJ’s two 25bps rate hikes in December 2025 and Jun e 2026, 
which are feeding through via  lagged loan repricing  and 
expanding domestic net interest margins . Notably, the 
regulatory easing  of Japan banks’ exposure limits  for individual 
corporate groups grants megabanks greater flexibility to underwrite 
large-scale M&A and AI data centre transactions. While the near -
term earnings benefit is incremental, the medium -term implications 
are more meaningful, with greater flexibility to support loan growth.  
Europe banks  are also enter ing the  Q2 earnings season with a 
constructive set of catalysts. Net interest income is benefiting from a 
higher-for-longer rate path, and fee income is supported by resilient 
investment banking activity and a stabilising wealth management 
segment. Meanwhile, credit quality remains robust , with the 
European Banking Authority's June 2026 Risk Assessment showing 
non-performing loan ratios sitting at historic lows. Sustained capital 
distributions via dividends and share buybacks continue to be a key 
catalyst and represent a compelling risk-adjusted return opportunity. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

The technology sector is expected to continue 
leading the US market in earnings growth over the 
next 12 months

Standard Chartered Bank

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What might be the implications of next week’s  Fed, BoJ 
and BoE policy meetings for bond and FX markets? 
Our view: We expect the Fed, BoJ and BoE to keep policy rates 
unchanged. Guidance will be key: i) a neutral Fed should limit 
further gains in US bond yields and USD, ii) BoJ is expected to 
keep the door open to further policy normalisation; and iii) the 
BoE is likely to retain a cautious inflation bias. 
Rationale: We expect the Fed to hold rates at 3.5-3.75%. Softer 
June US consumer inflation  supports a hold, although sustained 
strength in WTI oil prices could reinforce upside inflation risks. 
A neutral message w ill likely cap short -term gains in government 
bond yields and reduce support for the USD, while renewed 
emphasis on inflation risks would lift yields elevated. The USD Index 
(DXY) is likely to remain rangebound between 100.3 and 102.  
The BoJ is likely to hold its policy rate at 1.00% next week , but 
the weak JPY and imported inflation should preserve the possibility 
of further normalisation. Reports suggest some policymakers see 
scope for faster policy normalisation if JPY weakness, higher fuel 
costs and quicker cost pass -through push inflation above 
projections. Combined with our expectation that the Fed  will remain 
on hold, this should eventually allow USD/JPY to ease. Technically, 
we believe the USD/JPY correction is likely to be gradual rather than 
immediate. FX intervention and higher pension fund allocations 
to domestic assets may slow JPY depreciation, but a sustained 
reversal will require a narrower US-Japan interest rate differential. A 
sustained move below 160 will bring the 155-158 range into focus. 
We expect another hold by BoE on 30 July, following June’s 7-2 vote 
to hold rates at 3.75%. Persistent energy-driven inflation could keep 
UK Gilt yields elevated and support GBP, although weak activity and 
fiscal uncertainty may limit gains. GBP/USD is likely to stay 
rangebound between 1.3200 and 1.3500.   
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
 Will the PBoC implement monetary easing measures , 
given China’s weak Q2 GDP? Will this affect the CNH outlook? 
Our view: A reserve requirement ratio (RRR) cut remains 
possible, but any resulting CNH depreciation should be limited.  
Rationale: China’s economy grew 4.7% in H1, in line with the 
government’s annual growth target, although Q2 GDP moderated to 
4.3% y/y from 5.0%  in Q1 . Industrial production and exports 
remained resilient . Overall macroeconomic conditions do not 
suggest a urgent need for broad-based monetary easing. An RRR 
cut could weigh on the CNH initially by reinforcing concerns over 
wider policy divergence with the US. However, such a cut primarily 
releases banking system liquidity and does not necessarily signal 
a shift towards aggressive monetary easing. We therefore expect 
any CNH weakness to remain  measured, supported by China’s 
external balance and policymakers’ preference for exchange -rate 
stability. USD/CNH has near term support at 6.75. 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist

## Document 310: Is China’s Surplus Really Shrinking_ (2026-07-27)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Is China’s Surplus Really Shrinking_ (2026-07-27).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-07-27
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
Is China’s Surplus Really Shrinking?
Look past the gold imports, and China’s trade surplus is still climbing.
PUBLISHED
July 27, 2026 1:49 p.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
The Economist is now arguing that China’s trade
surplus has peaked.
That is a strange argument, as the “real” (price-
adjusted) trade surplus is still growing. Net exports
have contributed positively to China’s growth in Q1

and Q2.*
But it also isn’t an argument that stands up too much
scrutiny. It goes away with a single adjustment.
Chip prices are up, and China imports a ton of chips.
But that isn’t in fact the needed adjustment. China’s
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
exports of chips are up almost as much as its imports.
China imports a lot of oil but that too isn’t the needed
adjustment. The price of oil imports is up but import
volumes have famously collapsed; the commodity
import bill is up, but only modestly.
The critical adjustment is to net out imports of gold
—year-to-date imports in 2026 are $146 billion,
nearly $100 billion above the 2025 H1 total. In q2,
gold imports were an incredible 1.5 pp of GDP, so

the gold deficit now exceeds
Without that import surge, the overall goods trade
surplus would be up by over $80 billion in the first
half of 2026.
Yes, there are changes in the commodity balance and
the manufacturing balance too. See the “raw”
numbers from China’s customs, which haven’t been
adjusted for gold.

### Page 3

3
But gold is the critical adjustment to the headline
number, not chips or oil (to my surprise to be
honest).** The surge in imports, over a period when
gold prices are down, has been massive.
Facts are pesky things.

### Page 4

4
The underlying Chinese surplus is still heading up,
just as the underlying U.S. deficit is now expanding
(because of “AI”/ data center investment and a big
fiscal deficit).***
The easiest way to see this is to look at Chinese trade
in manufactures excluding chips and excluding gold
(refined gold is classified as a manufacture, the
commodity is gold rich ores).
Exports are up. This shouldn’t be a surprise: auto
exports are absolutely booming, and imports are still

flat.
This isn’t to say that chips aren’t important—China
runs a deficit there, and that deficit expanded just a
bit.
But the main driver of the increase in China’s
reported manufactured imports was the rise in chip

### Page 5

5
prices, which has clearly been huge, and that factor
also inflates exports.
And all this is more dramatic when examined in
higher frequency numbers. Without the surge in gold
imports the overall surplus would have been up
significantly in the second quarter even with the (big)
rise in chip prices.

### Page 6

6
There is a secondary point here.
Chinese import and export prices didn’t move much
in the first quarter, and then jumped in the second
quarter. China’s statistical authorities were slow to
recognize the “chip” shock and the broad impact of
higher memory chip prices in particular.

### Page 7

7
This did end up distorting China’s import and export
volume data in the first quarter (if the dollar value of
trade rises and import and export prices don’t rise,
statistically there is an increase in the volume of
trade).
This reduced China’s reported Q1 growth (the net
export contribution should have been bigger). And
because gold is in the Q2 data in a big way (offsetting
the fall in oil imports), true import in Q2 were likely
overstated… and that pulled down reported growth a

bit as well.
All these adjustments though are complex. The
easiest way to see what is happening is just to focus on
the manufacturing surplus without gold and chips. It
keeps on rising.
In fact, without the 1.5 pp of GDP in gold imports in
q2, the goods surplus would be close to 7% of GDP --

### Page 8

8
an absolutely massive number for an economy as big
as China.
The trend is clear. Too bad the financial press missed
it.****
*The NX contribution, which is the change in the
“real” trade surplus, was +0.8 pp of GDP in Q1 and
+0.9 pp of GDP in Q2.

### Page 9

9
** The composition of China’s imports has evolved
rapidly in the last few months. The chip price surge
has had a huge impact. So I was expecting it to have a
bigger impact on the “net” than what actually shows
up in the numbers. This is a case where rigorous
analysis that decomposes both exports and imports is
needed.

### Page 10

10
This work represents the views solely of the author(s). The
Council on Foreign Relations is an independent,
nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and
publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters
of policy.
From Follow the Money and Greenberg Center for Geoeconomics
Creative Commons: Some rights reserved.
This work is licensed under Creative Commons Attribution-
NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International (CC BY-NC-
ND 4.0) License.

CHINA ECONOMICS TRADE
*** The 2025 U.S. trade data also needs to be gold-
adjusted, by the way. The threat of tariffs had a huge
impact on the Q1 data, and reversal of the flood of
imported gold bars (which are in manufactures…)
was a big reason for the relatively low U.S. trade
deficit in Q4 and Q1.
**** The rapid increase in auto export volumes, both
globally and with respect to Europe, should have
given the Economist pause before it embraced the

arguments of Adam Wolfe of Absolute Strategy
Research. Too many China analysts are trying to
obscure what should be obvious: China continues to
rely heavily on exports for growth.

## Document 314: June CPI – better than feared (2026-08-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/June CPI – better than feared (2026-08-01).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#inflation #australia #rba #economicbulletinsaustralia#inflation
June CPI – better than feared
By Neha Sharma
Economist, Westpac
16:28 July 29 2026 Share
June Quarter CPI: Headline 0.6%qtr/3.9%yr; Trimmed Mean 0.8%qtr/3.6%yr. June Monthly
CPI: Headline –0.1%mth/3.8%yr, Trimmed Mean 0.3%mth/3.6%yr.
June quarter CPI rose 0.6%qtr, below both Westpac and market expectations of 0.7%qtr, while
annual headline inflation eased to 3.9%yr.

### Page 2

The CPI rose 0.6% in the June quarter, below both Westpac's forecast and market expectations of
0.7%qtr. As a result, annual headline inflation eased to 3.9%yr. The moderation in headline inflation
largely reflected the temporary halving of the fuel excise over the quarter, which saw automotive fuel
prices fall –2.1%qtr. Weakness elsewhere was driven by domestic travel and urban transport fares.

The trimmed mean (TM) rose 0.8% in the June quarter, taking the annual pace to 3.6% from 3.5% in
the March quarter. This was also below Westpac's forecast and market expectations of 0.9%qtr and
3.7%yr. The quarterly TM pace slowed at the margin, printing at 0.81%qtr in the June quarter versus
0.84% in the March quarter. The result was also below the RBA's profile in its May Statement on
Monetary Policy, which had annual TM inflation at 3.8%yr.

Trimmed mean inflation printed 0.8%qtr and 3.6%yr, again below our and the market's expectations.
The quarterly momentum slowed slightly from the March quarter and we saw the share of items
running above 3%yr narrow.
The June monthly CPI printed –0.1%mth and 3.8%yr. The result was also weaker than we expected
as downside risks around holiday travel and seasonal discounting were materialised. The monthly
trimmed mean inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.6%yr.
While domestic inflation pressures remain elevated, the result was less concerning than feared and
reduces the risk of a renewed acceleration in inflation. As such, we no longer expect the RBA to
deliver further rate hikes this year.

### Page 3

Market services excluding volatile items (3mth average) came in at 0.5%qtr and 3.3%yr, with the year-
ended pace holding steady from the March quarter.

We also saw a narrowing in the breadth of inflation, with the share of the basket running above 3%yr
easing to 42% from 45% in the March quarter and 46% in the December quarter. We also saw the
share running below 2%yr tick higher.

Overall, the June quarter data were less concerning than we had anticipated. We highlighted some key
risks in our preview – in particular, that holiday travel and areas that see the usual end-of-financial-year
discounting could come in weaker-than-expected, with stronger reads for housing and domestic market
services being a potential offset. In the event, only the downside risks materialised.

As Chief Economist Luci Ellis detailed earlier today, we no longer expect the RBA to deliver further rate
hikes this year. The sizeable pass-through from higher energy costs evident in the early phase of the
Middle East conflict has not been sustained, reducing the risk of a renewed inflation acceleration. A
November rate hike remains a possibility if inflation surprises to the upside in Q3, but it is no longer our
central expectation.

### Page 4

Today’s result suggests downside risks to our broader inflation profile. We had expected some strength
in the back half of the year, some of this relating to the ongoing impact from the Middle East conflict,
particularly from fertilizer prices, but also the impact of weather events and the higher minimum wage
outcome. We will revisit these in an upcoming bulletin once we have processed all the data, accounting
for the lower starting point.

June month in more detail (all monthly figures are non-seasonally adjusted unless stated):
The June CPI printed –0.1%mth, below both our forecast and market expectations of 0.4%mth and
0.2%mth, respectively. TM inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.6%yr, also softer than our forecast of 0.4%mth
and 3.7%yr.

The chief culprit behind the decline in the month was automotive fuel, which fell 10.9%mth and
subtracted 0.4ppts from headline inflation. Other sources behind the monthly weakness were clothing
& footwear, household furnishings, and audio, visual and computing equipment. These saw the usual
end-of-financial-year discounting and also eased on a year-on-year basis.

### Page 5

Partly offsetting the above, was a 12.7%mth rise in urban transport fares and a 4.6%mth increase in
holiday travel. For the latter, most of this reflected a seasonal increase with domestic travel falling –
1.0%mth and international rising 0.3%mth, suggesting conditions here remain benign and are not
reflecting earlier pricing pressures from the Middle East conflict.

Market services excluding volatiles and holiday travel was flat in the month, with annual inflation here
at 4.4%yr. Insurance was notably weak, with prices falling 0.3%mth and annual inflation easing to
4.9%yr. The softness in the month was somewhat surprising given replacement and repair costs for
housing and motor vehicles continue to record solid gains.

In terms of the key surprises:

Upside surprises
Accessories rose 4.0%mth, well above our 0.4%mth forecast.
Food inflation was slightly firmer, driven by non-alcoholic beverages (1.0%mth vs –0.6%mth) and
dairy (1.3%mth vs 0.1%mth). Takeaway also surprised to the upside (0.6%mth vs 0.0%mth). Partly
offseting these was a softer outcome for fruit & vegetables (1.0%mth vs 2.0%mth).

### Page 6

Downside surprises

Spare parts for motor vehicles were up 0.7%mth vs –0.5%mth, while repair costs rose 0.3%mth vs –
0.3%mth.
Holiday travel was the key downside surprise, rising 4.6%mth vs 8.1%mth.
Housing was broadly in line in the quarter, though both new dwelling costs (0.4%mth vs 0.7%mth)
and rents (0.3%mth vs 0.4%mth) came in slightly softer than expected in June.
Audio, visual and computing equipment and services fell 1.4%mth, well below our expectation of a
0.1% decline.
Household textiles fell 2.9%mth, compared with our expectation of a flat outcome.

### Page 7

The world is facing yet another major climate shock with the incoming El Niño

Most climate events are operational concerns and this one has the potential to become a macroeconomic event.  
The IMF estimates that past El Niño episodes raised global food prices by roughly 5% over a year and lifted headline 
inflation by between 0.1
 -
1pp  
across affected economies, with the effect rising in line with the weight of food in the 
consumer basket
 1
. Going further, Callahan & Mankin (2023)
 2 
attribute some USD4.1trn and USD5.7trn in cumulative 
global income losses to the 1982
 -

 -
98 events 
 – 
concentrated in the tropics, where economies such as 
Peru and Indonesia ran more than 10% below trend several years later 
 – 
and put a possible US
 D3trn drag on global 
output through 2029 on the 2023 event alone. These are upper
 -
bound, still
 -
debated estimates, but they frame the 
order of magnitude: this is an inflation and income event, not merely an agricultural one.

Three factors make the current outlook particularly important.  
First, the probability of a major event is now high 
and rising. NOAA's Climate Prediction Center upgraded its outlook to an El Niño Advisory in June 2026 and assigns 
around a 63% chance of a very strong event by November
 -
January 
 – 
one it judges would rank  
among the largest in 
the record since 1950; the WMO puts the probability near 80% and the World Bank cited a 61
 -
87% chance of 
emergence by mid
 -
2026, persisting into 2027. Second, several agricu
 ltural markets enter the event with limited 
buffers. Inventories in commodities such as cocoa, palm oil and rice are already tighter than historical norms, 
reducing the system's ability to absorb supply disruptions. Third, governments have become more inte
 rventionist 
since previous El Niño cycles. Export restrictions, strategic stockpiling and domestic price controls are now common 
policy tools. These interventions can amplify the economic impact of weather shocks far beyond the initial 
production losses. T
 he result is that relatively modest supply disruptions can rapidly become significant economic 
events.

What past El Niños  
did to global catastrophe patterns  
Using a record of some 9,400 events since 2000 we explore how 
 El Niño affects NatCat events distribution 
globally.  
Each event was tagged to an ENSO phase using NOAA’s Oceanic Niño Index episode record, and we 
measured how the frequency of each hazard and each region shifts in El Niño years relative to neutral ones
 3
. The 
exercise is validated by a placebo: earthquakes and volcanoes, which have no link to ocean temperature, show no 
El Niño signal (1.0×), which is what allows the elevated hazard ratios to be read as signal rather than an artefact of 
rising reporting ov
 er time.

El Niño rewires the mix of catastrophes rather than lifting them across the board (Figure 2).  
Drought (1.6×) and 
extreme heat (1.2×) rise clearly, floods more modestly (1.1×), while storm frequency is essentially unchanged 
globally (1.0×). That last point is the important one: El Niño redistributes cyclone activity rather than adding to it. 
Wildfi
 re actually sits below its neutral rate worldwide, because its signal is regional and washes out in the global 
total. In the two strongest past events (the closest  
analogues to an expected strong 2026
 -

intensify further still.

Where could we see most disruptions?  
The strongest impacts are expected across Southeast Asia, India and parts of West Africa. 
 These regions sit at 
the center of global production for several critical food commodities. Any further reduction in output could renew 
volatility in commodity markets and increase pressure on food product manufacturers. India is the world's largest 
rice e
xporter and a major producer of sugar. A weaker
 -
than
 -
normal monsoon could reduce agricultural output and 
place renewed pressure on domestic food prices. Historically,  
the government's response has often been to restrict 
exports in order to protect domestic consumers. Such decisions have an outsized impact: India alone accounts for 
roughly 40% of global rice trade. The 2023 episode is instructive. As El Niño concerns bu
 ilt, India banned non
 -
basmati white rice exports in July 2023, and benchmark Thai prices jumped more than 20% within weeks; the FAO 
rice price index rose almost 10% month
 -
on
-
month in August, a 15
 -
year high
 4
. For multinational businesses, Indian 
policy decisions may prove more important than the weather itself.

Indonesia, Malaysia, Thailand and Vietnam are exposed to drought risk during major El Niño events. 
 The region 
is critical for palm oil, sugar, rice and coffee. Disruptions in any of these markets can quickly affect food 
manufacturers, consumer
 -
goods companies and global retailers. Palm oil deserves particular attention because of 
its importance across p
 rocessed foods, personal care products and industrial supply chains.

Fallout in Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana could disrupt global cocoa production.  
The cocoa market enters this event 
with already constrained inventories and structural production challenges. The most recent El Niño shows how

Higher 
 crops expected in Latin America, not necessarily good news

One of the most misunderstood aspects of El Niño is that it does not create uniform disruption.  
In fact, some 
regions are likely to benefit. IMF research confirms this asymmetry directly: a typical El Niño shaves activity in 
Australia, Chile, India, Indonesia, Japan, New Zealand and South Africa, while the US tends to gain directly and 
Europe indirec
 tly, through positive trade spillovers.
 6

Brazil and Argentina may emerge as beneficiaries in yields but not necessarily in economic terms. 
 Historically, 
El Niño improves growing conditions across some agricultural regions, supporting: soybean, corn, and sugar 
output in parts of Latin America. Stronger harvests have the potential to improve trade balances, support 
economic activity and provide  
a natural offset to inflationary pressures. Argentina's grain sector also typically 
benefits from the wetter conditions associated with El Niño.  El Niño's effect on Brazil is regionally split 
 – 
supportive for southern crops, but historically associated w
 ith drought in the north and the Amazon basin 
 – 
and 
much of Argentina's upside reflects a rebound from the severe La Niña drought that preceded it, rather than an 
absolute windfall. However, higher production could also put downward pressure on prices whil
 e demand is 
rather inelastic (i.e. consumption of food in volume do not increase significantly when prices increase). Lastly, 
when there is a bit of oversupply, inventory facilities fills up rapidly and prices increase steeply 
 – 
another 
headwind for farmer
 s and corporates looking for storage.

Parts of the US often experience favorable agricultural conditions during major El Niño events.  
Corn and 
soybean production have historically performed well, providing an important counterweight to disruptions 
elsewhere. The implication is important, the world is unlikely to experience a broad
 -
based food shortage. Instead, 
supply will become increas
 ingly uneven across regions and commodities.

The Northern Hemisphere is mostly a beneficiary.  
Strong El Niños raise wind shear over the Atlantic and hold 
hurricane activity down. NOAA's 2026 outlook puts the odds of a below
 -
normal Atlantic season at 55% and 
forecasts 8 to 14 named storms versus a norm of 14; CSU now sees 11 named storms, 5 hurrica
 nes and 2 major.
 7 
Fewer Atlantic landfalls means lower insured catastrophe losses and fewer Gulf oil, gas and LNG outages. 
Northern US winters also tend to run mild because the cold Canadian air stays bottled up, which trims heating 
demand: the difference between a cold wi
 nter and a mild El Niño one is roughly 4 Bcf/d
 8 
of US gas demand, about 
5%, and the last episode pushed Henry Hub to multi
 -
year lows. So for the North the price signal leans 
disinflationary, the opposite of the food story in Asia.

E
nergy, power and freight  
could also be disrupted, and governments take action  
El Niño does not only affect food.  
The droughts it brings to Latin America and parts of Asia curtail hydropower 
and, as the IMF notes, raise demand for coal and crude oil to fill the gap in thermal generation; offsetting this, 
milder Northern
 -
Hemisphere winters can soften heating
 -
gas deman
 d. Net energy effects are therefore regional 
and partly self
 -
cancelling, but the power
 -
generation channel is a real source of price and fiscal pressure in hydro
 -
dependent economies.  
The supply
 -
chain impact also runs through shipping chokepoints.  
In 2023
 -
24, an El Niño
 -
driven drought forced 
the Panama Canal, a route for around 6% of global trade, to cut daily transits from a normal 36 to as few as 24 
and impose draft limits that reduced cargo loads; queues swelled to roughly 160 vessels and full
 -
y
ear transits fell 
29%, with LNG shipments down by two
 -
thirds as carriers rerouted around Africa. With NOAA already flagging the 
canal as a watch
 -
point for 2026
 -
27, freight and routing risk belo
 ngs on the corporate exposure map alongside 
commodity prices.  
History suggests we should pay attention to policymakers.  
During previous food
 -
price shocks, government 
intervention frequently became the dominant market driver. Potential policy responses include export restrictions, 
strategic stockpile accumulation, price controls, import subsidies and tariff adjustments. Ind
 ia's interventions on 
rice markets is a textbook illustration. A modest production shortfall can trigger export restrictions. Those 
restrictions then affect importing countries across Asia, Africa an
 d the Middle East. Importing governments 
respond with emergency purchases, amplifying price movements further. In Asia, India, Thailand and Vietnam, as 
major rice and agricultural exporters, are the most likely to do so. While export restrictions could eas
 e domestic 
supply pressures and give local central banks more flexibility, they would raise inflation risks for regional food 
importers, especially the Philippines and Indonesia.

### Page 7

7

The economic impact becomes far larger than the original weather event. For global businesses, this policy 
feedback loop often creates greater uncertainty than the agricultural disruption itself.  
Southeast Asia  
and some parts of Latin America 
 may face inflationary pressure

Inflation has already hit consumers across ASEAN and India following the Iran war.  
With the Philippines most 
affected initially as headline inflation reached +7.2% y/y in April. While second
 -
round effects are expected to lift 
inflation modestly across the region, Thailand remains an exception, with inflation still below target due to 
de
flationary pressures. Energy
 -
price pressure has eased after the gradual reopening of the Strait of Hormuz, though 
prices remain above pre
 -
crisis levels. Current 2026 inflation forecasts are 4.5% for India, 3.8% for Indonesia, 2.3% for 
Malaysia, 5.2% for th
 e Philippines, 2.0% for Thailand and 4.8% for Vietnam. In 2027, inflation is expected to 
normalize to 4.0%, 3.0%, 1.5%, 3.6%, 1.1% and 3.2%, respectively.  
A new inflation risk for 2027 now comes from a potential super El Niño, mainly through food prices.  
Exposure 
depends on food’s weight in CPI baskets: Malaysia is least exposed at around 30%, followed by Indonesia and 
Vietnam at around 35%, Thailand and India at around 40%, and the Philippines above 45%. Given these exposures, 
a strong El Niño would larg
 ely undo the disinflationary gains from the reopening of Hormuz for 2026 and push 
inflation higher into 2027.  
Drawing on the 2015
 –
2016 El Niño precedent, we 
 expect Indonesia to be the most 
affected, with a cumulated impact of +2.3pp, followed by Thailand, Vietnam and India at approximately +1.3pp 
each, while Malaysia and the Philippines appear more insulated at around +0.7pp.  
Figure 5: Inflation across ASEAN and India in 2026
 -
2027

Sources: IMF, Allianz Research  
Policy responses across ASEAN and India are likely to diverge, as central banks start from different positions.  
Indonesia and the Philippines have already tightened policy, raising rates by 100bps and 50bps, respectively. The 
RBI is expected to begin hiking in H2 with two rate increases, while Malaysia, Thailand and, to a lesser extent, 
Vietnam have remained on hol
 d.

If El Niño materializes at full strength, the Philippines, Indonesia and India may need to hike again or delay 
easing until 2027. 
 Malaysia and Thailand should stay on hold but remain ready to act. For Thailand, a severe El 
Niño would likely rule out rate cuts to support growth, which had already been largely postponed due to the Iran 
war.

episodes
 ,

electricity prices 
 (especially for Columbia)

 forex depreciations.

Companies with exposure to food ingredients should prepare for renewed input
 -
cost volatility. 
 The highest
 -
risk 
commodities include sugar, palm oil, cocoa and rice. Margin management may become more challenging if 
inflation
 -
sensitive consumers resist further price increases.  
Companies with sourcing exposure to Southeast Asia should review supply
 -
chain resilience and inventory 
strategies.  
The risk is less about physical shortages and more about cost volatility and policy intervention.  
Credit stress could emerge among food processors, agricultural exporters and businesses operating in 
vulnerable emerging markets.  
Conversely, agricultural exporters in Brazil and other beneficiary regions may 
experience stronger operating conditions.  
Super El Niño events should not be considered as a potential driver for major market turbulences. 
 For developed 
markets, the signal is essentially absent at the index level: the S&P 500 has delivered strong positive raw returns in 
every super El Niño window in our sample, averaging +30% over the 18 months following an ONI peak 
 - 
not because 
El Niño hel
 ps the US economy, but because these episodes have historically coincided with US mid
 -
cycle expansions 
or early Fed easing cycles, which dominate any weather
 -
related noise. The global business cycle, monetary policy, 
and earnings revisions are orders of ma
 gnitude more important for US markets than El Niño. For emerging markets, 
with the exception of 2015
 -
2016, there seems to be a pattern of equity underperformance relative to global 
benchmarks, concentrated in food
 -
importing, drought
 -
exposed economies (the 
 Philippines and Indonesia most 
clearly), and it tends to peak 12
 -
18 months after the ONI peak rather than coinciding with it. But we must underline 
that history only counts three to four observations per country, each episode unfolded against a radically d
 ifferent 
macroeconomic backdrop (i.e. the Asian financial crisis in 1997
 -
98, the China slowdown in 2015
 -
16, and the post
 -
COVID inflation cycle in 2023
 -
24) and in several cases these confounding factors dwarf anything El Niño could 
plausibly explain indepen
 dently. What our event study identifies is more accurately described as a tendency for 
ENSO
 -
exposed EM markets to underperform in an environment that also tends to be globally risk
 -
off, rather than 
a clean causal impact of El Niño on stock prices. The comm
 odity and food
 -
inflation transmission are real and well
 -
identified, their translation into markets underperformance is plausible and directionally consistent, but the signal 
is weak, heterogeneous, and not separable from global macro with only a few events
 . 
What we need to monitor going forward  
1. Indian monsoon performance. 
 The June
 -
September 2026 monsoon season will provide the clearest indication 
of risks to rice and sugar markets.

2. Indonesian and Malaysian production data. 
 Palm oil inventories will offer one of the earliest indicators of 
whether supply conditions are tightening.

3. West African cocoa harvests. 
 Evidence of continued production weakness would reinforce concerns about 
already
 -
tight cocoa markets.

4. Government intervention. 
 Export restrictions, stockpiling programs and food
 -
security measures should be 
treated as leading indicators of broader market disruption.

5. Inflation expectations. 
 Food
 -
price inflation often appears in consumer expectations before it is fully visible in 
official prints. Monitoring inflation expectations may therefore provide earlier warning than headline CPI data.

## Document 383: What a _Strong and Rich Japan_ could mean (2026-07-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What a _Strong and Rich Japan_ could mean (2026-07-24).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-07-24
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

What a "Strong and 
Rich Japan" could mean

1 
Authors 
Mallika Sachdeva 
Strategist

July 24, 2026 
Executive summary 
— Japan could be at the precipice of a very significant shift in fiscal and 
industrial policy, with echoes of the Meiji Restoration. Japan’s new economic 
blueprint seeks to put the country on a path of investment-driven growth to 
meet the challenges of a new era.  
— Japan is not alone in its bid to prioritize industry and rearm, but given a higher 
starting point for government debt, it will have to find room to spend while 
maintaining fiscal sustainability. This will inform Japan’s approach in very 
significant ways.  
— First, Japan is likely to remain focused on supporting nominal GDP growth, 
aiming to keep it above government funding costs to create deficit space. 
Second, Japan will look to actively mobilize domestic savings to fund 
investments and manage yield pressure. Each of these shifts could have 
implications for the currency.  
— Japan’s excess savings have arguably been kept in two places: in cash and 
abroad. The government is looking to get both moving. Households have 
around 50% of their $15tn of savings in cash and deposits. Meanwhile, GPIF 
has 50% of its $1.8tn portfolio abroad. If industry, energy, defence need to 
come home, so might the capital to fund them. 
— Over the past few months, Japanese policy makers have been very focused on 
stabilizing the currency. However, if fiscal capacity is becoming the most 
important policy criteria, incentives could shift from FX to yield management.  
— The ultimate impact on USD/JPY will depend on the levers the government 
chooses to pull to manage yields. If GPIF is mandated to bring money back 
into domestic assets, this could be very bullish for the JPY . However, if the BoJ 
is coopted to support bonds through renewed JGB purchases, this could be 
very negative for the JPY .  
— Suppressing yield volatility could come alongside bigger moves in FX from 
here, depending on the approach the government takes. This favours higher 
JPY vol.

### Page 2

24 July 2026 
FX Special Report

Deutsche Bank AG Page 2

Echoes of Meiji  
Japan could be at the precipice of a very significant shift in fiscal and industrial 
policy, with echoes of the Meiji Restoration. In 1868, pre -modern Japan was 
staring at the threat of foreign involvement and  colonization, having seen the 
Opium Wars  unfold in China. Japan had signed unequal trade treaties that 
opened Japanese ports to free trade imperialism, prevented Japan from setting 
tariffs, and exempted foreigners from local laws. Japan’s response changed 
history: under the Meiji Restoration, Japan retu rned rule to the emperor, 
strengthened the state, and pursued extremely rapid industrialization.  
One could argue that Japan again faces existential challenges given fractures in 
globalization, intensifying great power competition, insecurity around energy and 
critical minerals, the need to participate in AI innovation, and external 
dependence for defence. Japan’s answer under PM Takaichi: to return to its roots 
as an industrial nation driven by state and fiscal policy. The slogan of the 1868 
Meiji Restoration was “Fukoku Kyohei” which translates into “Enrich the Country, 
Strengthen the Armed Forces” or more simply: rich country, s trong army. It is 
perhaps telling that Takaichi’s government is introducing an economic blueprint 
that calls for a “Strong and Rich Japan”. (Nikkei Asia).  
Invest, invest, invest  
Japan’s Cabinet has now passed the  Honebuto, or the government’s new Basic 
Policy on Economic and Fiscal Management. The document calls for moving 
beyond “conventional thinking”, “putting an end to the trend of 
underinvestment”, and “responding to the era of intense global industrial policy 
competition”. The cornerstone of Japan’s proposed policy shift seems to be 
Y370trillion of public -private partnership investments with the government 
having identified 17 key strategic areas from AI and quantum, to defence, 
aviation, shipbuilding, critical minerals, and others. These investments are seen 
as crucial to boosting potential growth. Indeed, the decline in Japan’s potential 
growth rate has not principally been about demographics as is commonly 
assumed, but about the collapse in the capital stock  (Figure 1). Japan went 
through decades of very low investment after the 1990 asset bubble burst. It now 
wants to return to being an investment-driven economy.  
Finding the funding: two big shifts  
Japan is not alone in its bid to reindustrialize and rearm, but compared to peers, 
Japan arguably has less fiscal space. Japan’s debt to GDP hovers above 200%, 
while Germany and Korea are under 50%, making it easier for them to pursue 
significant expansion  in their fiscal deficits. While Germany is moving from 
austerity to expansion, Japan will have to find spending room while continuing to 
chart a fiscally sustainable path. This will hugely inform Japan’s policy approach. 
There are two important shifts Japan appears to be making to enable fiscal 
spending. 
First, changing the fiscal target. The Honebuto has confirmed a shift  in Japan’s 
core fiscal target away from the primary balance. The primary balance will now no 
longer be a mechanical target; it will be managed over many years, and be allowed 
to deteriorate temporarily as required. The new fiscal target is  now the “stable 
reduction of the total debt-to-GDP ratio for national and local governments.” The 
second notable and enabling shift is the pursuit of an “asset management nation” 
strategy. Japan is looking to more actively mobilize domestic savings to fund

### Page 3

24 July 2026 
FX Special Report

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

investments and help keep government financing costs manageable. Each of 
these shifts could have significant long-term implications for the currency.  
The new fiscal approach means keeping r below g  
We think debt sustainability arithmetic has become key to understanding Japan’s 
economic policy. Recall that: Δdt ≈ (r−g)dt-1 +pdt where d is the debt-to-GDP ratio, 
pd is the annual primary deficit, g is the nominal GDP growth rate, and r is the 
average interest rate paid on outstanding debt. The formula implies that Japan 
can run primary deficits and still achieve a declining debt-to-GDP ratio, as long as 
r is below g. In other words, Japan’s cost of funding needs to be below nominal 
GDP growth for Japan to have fiscal deficit space. One might consider 
Abenomics’ bid to return Japan to inflation as crucial to putting it on a path to 
fiscal health. As Figure 2 illustrates, the peaking in Japan’s debt -GDP ratios has 
gone hand-in-hand with the revival in Japan’s nominal GDP.

Figure 1:  Japanese potential growth has declined on 
decades of underinvestment – this could now change

Figure 2: Rising nominal GDP has helped bring down 
debt-to-GDP

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bank of Japan

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics

The extent of fiscal deficit space will crucially depend on the interplay between r 
and g with  Figure 3 illustrating the potential space crea ted by different (r -g) 
scenarios. We note that the Honebuto outlined a 3% nominal GDP target (g) for 
Japan till 2040, made up of a 2% inflation target, and 1% real growth target, with 
aims of increasing it. This perhaps sheds light on why the government has 
become sensitive to the 3% level on 10Y yields . To be sure, Japan’s overall cost 
of funding remains much lower for now at near 1% given just a fraction of debt is 
refinanced every year , but there are points of vulnerability. While the weighted 
average maturity of debt outstanding is around 9 years, BoJ holds close to 50% 
of outstanding JGBs against which they pay interest on excess reserves to banks. 
This means that almost half the consolidated liability base is in fact linked to front-
end floating rates. Therefore, not only are long-run government bond yields above 
3% not likely to be  sustainable, front -end rate hikes also contribute to 
consolidated fiscal costs. Very roughly, every 100bps of rate hikes costs Y5tn or 
around 0.7% of GDP. 
The (r-g) formula not only explains the importance of understanding the drivers 
of ‘r’, but also explain s the sensitivity to ‘g’. It helps one appreciate why 
policymakers remain so worried about upsetting the balance on hard -won 
nominal GDP growth. Indeed, Japan tends to react very cautiously and dovishly

### Page 4

24 July 2026 
FX Special Report

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

to any signs of growth risks (tariffs, Iran war) and has preferred to let inflation run 
hot and durable before tightening.  
Japanese policy makers face a fine balancing act in optimizing this formula:  
dovish monetary policy may help to support g, but is also driving up r via inflation 
expectations reflected in long -run bond yields . Conversely, hawkish monetary 
policy could hurt ‘g’ and also raise the cost of ‘r’ via reserve payments. In light of 
this, the focus on trying to manage long-end yields makes more sense. 
How might Japan do this?  Changes to  supply-side of debt  management have 
been tried via reduced long-end issuance; the government may now be trying to 
more actively influence demand.  
An Asset Management Nation 
Very simply: Japan needs to find more buyers of JGBs ( Figure 4). It is natural to 
look at where its own domestic capital has been invested. The answer points to 
two big places: in cash and overseas. Japan has close to USD5tn in portfolio 
assets held abroad across central bank, household, pension fund, insurance, and 
bank balance sheets, more than the size of GDP. Indeed, our research has lon g 
shown that one of the best metrics of fiscal space is a country’s external position, 
and Japan may need to draw on it. Intuitively, the bid for strategic autonomy 
around the world should coincide with a growing “nationalism of capital.” If 
industry, energy, defence all need to come home, so might the capital to fund it. 
We have explored this in earlier research here. More of Japan’s money could now 
be needed at home. Finance Minister Katayama has been explicit that Japan’s 
ambitions to create a growth-oriented economy will coincide with a “strong stock 
market and positive interest rates” in which domestic citizens should reap 
benefits. The other perspective is they will help provide the money.

Figure 3: Keep g above r is key to Japan’s fiscal space. 
Japan does not want to return to the lost decades

Figure 4: Japan will need to find more buyers of JGBs

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Ministry of Finance

Where the money could come from  
Pension fund and  household assets appear to be the two big areas of focus for 
markets. The government pension fund, GPIF, currently has 50% of its $1.8tn 
portfolio abroad. Meanwhile , households have around 50% of their $15tn (!)  of 
savings in cash and deposits (Figure 5).

### Page 5

24 July 2026 
FX Special Report

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5

Movement of household deposits into JGBs could be encouraged through a few 
channels. JGBs may be brought under the NISA tax-exempt investment scheme; 
the retail JGB offering may be expanded or made more attractive. There were 
also references in the Honebuto to advancing on-chain finance, with the power of 
tokenization potentially being tapped to encourage financialization of sa vings. 
Mobilizing household savings could be material for yield management given their 
sheer size. For context, the 1000tn yen of household deposits are almost the size 
of the entire JGB market. It is not however likely to have any FX impact as it would 
mostly be an internal domestic flow. 
In contrast , a shift from GPIF to invest more in domestic assets could be very 
material for FX, with PM Takaichi noting this is a key initiative. GPIF’s current 
policy mix prescribes 25% investment in domestic equities and debt respectively, 
with +/ -6% bands around it. If GPIF simply shifted to the top end of current 
permissible ranges from current levels (as of March 2026), this could bring around 
$200bn back to Japan  across domestic equities ($130bn) and domestic bonds 
($75bn).  
The bigger shift to play for  however is an early policy review of GPIF’s overall 
asset mix that revises up the domestic asset targets themselves. In Figure 6 we 
illustrate scope for inflows at different levels of target allocation to domestic 
bonds alone. Hypothetically, if the target share of domestic bonds doubled to 
50%, over USD400bn could flow into JGBs from GPIF . W hile this  may seem 
extreme before Abenomics in 2012, GPIF had a 67% target allocation to domestic 
bonds in their policy mix. One could reasonably expect any flows to occur with a 
glide path, but the scale alone could be significant enough to impact the currency. 
We note that GPIF has a current return objective of a 1.9% real return above 
nominal wage growth. The extent of any domestic reallocation will depend on how 
much Japan’s domestic assets can deliver that return, or whether government 
pension returns are made subservient to financing costs for now.   Another group 
that may be called upon to play a bond absorption role could be local banks. This 
is a source of financial repression has been used extensively around the world and 
could return where deficit financing demands it.

Figure 5: Japanese households hold half their savings 
in cash and deposits

Figure 6: GPIF could be encouraged to bring more 
money back into domestic bonds

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, GPIF

### Page 6

24 July 2026 
FX Special Report

Deutsche Bank AG Page 6

A shift to yield management could lead to bigger FX moves  
Over the past few months, Japanese policy makers have arguably been solving 
for stabilizing the currency . Household angst and imported inflation risks have 
likely encouraged  this. Policymakers have engaged in verbal, rate check and 
actual intervention this year.  And, Japan has in fact been successful in keeping 
USD/JPY near 160 amid the US -Iran war, a major global energy shock, and 
volatility in Fed pricing.  
If, however, fiscal capacity is becoming the biggest and mo re existential policy 
criteria for Japan, then incentives may  be shifting from FX management to yield 
management: from capping USD/JPY to capping 10Y yields and borrowing costs.  
Suppressing interest rate volatility may come at the cost of amplifying it in FX , 
depending on the specific levers that the government chooses to pull . If GPIF is 
mandated to bring a larger share of their assets back into domestic assets, this 
would be very bullish for the JPY . We ha ve previously identified GPIF as the 
biggest weapon  in Japan ’s external arsenal. Equally, if  BoJ is encouraged to 
accelerate tightening to manage inflation risk premium in long-end yields this too 
could be bullish JPY . If, however, the BoJ is coopted to support yield 
management through a more active role in the bond market, potentially resuming 
JGB purchases, this would be negative for the JPY.  Similarly, if BoJ is 
encouraged to keep monetary policy dovish to prioritize (r-g) considerations this 
too would be bearish JPY.  
Ultimately, the direction of travel for USD/JPY will depend on which measures 
the government favours.  Clearer and more imminent signs of GPIF repatriation 
would favour the stronger yen tail, while pressure on BoJ to manage rates and 
yields again could open the weaker tail.  Efforts to suppress yield volatility could 
come alongside bigger moves in FX  from here. This favours higher JPY vols. We 
note USD/JPY vols are near multi-year lows with a 1Y USD/JPY straddle breaking 
even roughly below 150 or around 170.

Figure 7: Policy focus could be shifting from capping FX to capping yields

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP

What a "Strong and Rich Japan" could mean – Jul 24, 2026

Leopold 表⽰，为 7 ⽉基⾦资产暴跌 67% ⼀事 " 承担全部责任 " ，并承诺将汲取 " 代价惨重的教
训 " 。其基⾦将继续运营，不会退出公开股票市场，但今后不会再向银⾏借款以放⼤押

摘要：
这位被视为 "AI 股神 " 的对冲基⾦经理，在⼀场因 AI 股暴跌引发的强制清仓后，公开向投资者认错
并承诺浴⽕重⽣。
对冲基⾦ Situational Awareness 的创始⼈ Leopold Aschenbrenner 周四深夜致信投资者，为 7 ⽉
基⾦资产暴跌 67% ⼀事 " 承担全部责任 " ，并承诺将汲取 " 代价惨重的教训 " 。他表⽰，基⾦将继续
运营，不会退出公开股票市场，但今后不会再向银⾏借款以放⼤押注。尽管遭遇重创，该基⾦
今年仍录得约 80% 的正收益。
此前，⾯对融资⽅的追加保证⾦通知， Situational Awareness 紧急将⼤部分股票持仓出售给 Ken
Griffin 旗下的 Citadel ，折价幅度逾 10% 。该基⾦⽬前剩余资产组合（含私募投资）价值约 100 亿

美元。
强制清仓：从逾 200 亿美元到百亿美元
Situational Awareness 的溃败始于本周 AI 股的⼤规模抛售。该基⾦重仓押注 Bloom Energy 、
Sandisk 等⼀批与 AI 题材⾼度相关的股票，在市场动荡中遭到猛烈冲击。
据华尔街⽇报报道，随着损失累积，融资⽅相继发出追加保证⾦通知，基⾦被迫进⾏紧急资产
变现。周三，该基⾦与 Citadel 达成协议，以逾 10% 的折价出售了⼤部分公开股票持仓。
Aschenbrenner 在信中将这⼀局⾯⽐作 " 银⾏挤兑 " ，并指出基⾦在 " 与我们公开相关的股票名称上
遭遇了不利交易 " 。他同时将部分损失归咎于做空者蓄意针对基⾦持仓、进⼀步加剧跌幅。
据悉， Situational Awareness ⼀度与 Greenoaks 和 Sequoia Capital 牵头的财团谈妥，拟出售价
值 35 亿美元的私募 AI 巨头 Anthropic 股权，但据知情⼈⼠透露，该基⾦于周四上午反悔，最终撤
回了这笔交易。

⾼杠杆模式：激进押注的代价
Situational Awareness ⾃约两年前成⽴以来，资产管理规模迅速膨胀⾄逾 200 亿美元，成为近年
来扩张最快的对冲基⾦之⼀。
该基⾦的策略⾼度集中 —— 将资⾦押注在少数⼏只 AI 相关股票上，并通过向主经纪商借款⼤幅放
⼤头⼨。 Aschenbrenner 在创⽴该基⾦时并⽆任何专业投资经验，但凭借对 AI ⾏业的深度判断，
赢得了市场⼴泛关注，被部分投资者奉为 "AI 神谕 " ，其持仓动向⼀度被外界密切追踪。
今年前五个⽉，该基⾦累计涨幅约 270% 。然⽽，⾼杠杆在市场顺⻛时成倍放⼤收益，在逆⻛时
同样成倍放⼤亏损 ——7 ⽉的暴跌正是这⼀逻辑的直接体现。
承诺重建：去杠杆，再出发

重大破壞的風險 ，歐、亞洲回補庫存以因應冬季需
求預期，預估美國3Q26天然氣價格在3~3.4美元，
略優於於去年 。在歐洲，受中東天然氣恢復對亞洲
出口，TTF天然氣遠期價平均約 14.8美元/百萬英熱

單位 ，較上月降低 ，歐洲儲氣量雖拉升 ，但僅達
50%，遠低於過往 ，加上天氣過熱的降溫需求 ，預
期3Q26 TTF價格仍有上行空間。
重點摘要
• 天然氣：受歐 、亞洲天然氣
庫存回補需求及美 LNG出口
增加，預期3Q26美國天然氣
價格略優於去年同期 ，歐洲
因回補需求將推升天然氣價
格。
• 成品油受需求反彈快於供應 ，
超額利潤雖縮水 ，但仍維持
相對高位。
• 受油價回跌 ，市場 下修 能源
廠獲利 ，能源股 股價 受油價
疲軟壓抑。

《需求反彈快於供應，使美煉油廠仍擁超額利潤》
受煉油廠關廠 ，產能因戰事受損 ，成品油庫存短缺
風險高於原油 ，雖因荷姆茲海峽通航使油價大幅下
跌，但因汽油和柴油的需求反彈速度將快於供應 ，
將導致在更長時間內保持緊張 ，汽油和柴油價格回
落程度明顯低於原油 ，雖超額利潤縮水，但仍遠優
於過往平均 ，有利煉油廠 ，其下行風險在於川普 要

求煉油商降價。
《3Q26能源股獲利增，但股價受能源價跌壓抑》
美能源商雖受惠煉油裂解價差仍高及 LNG出口量增
加，但被油價回跌減少獲利抵消部分 ，預期市場將
下修美國能源商 獲利，但獲利因增產及成本下降而
成長，廠商維持資本紀律 ，使現金流仍強大 ，維持
股東回報 ，但因能源股股價與能源產品價格走勢相
關，對美國能源股持中立，較偏好煉油廠。

雖預期 2Q26商品化學受轉嫁原料成本上揚 ，將使

房市受高貸款利率影響仍疲軟 ，預估2Q26、3Q26
主要成長動能仍為定價力及預期3Q26成本下降抵銷
量持平或下滑 ，預估獲利年增率呈現持平或低個位

數成長。受尿素供給增加 、預期印度降雨量偏低將
降低尿素需求 ，尿素價格走跌 ，對磷酸鹽持觀望 ，
僅鉀肥價格持平，對美化肥商持中立。

受美國國內供給量有限 ，庫存偏低及美國鋼廠漲價 ，

《金價短期承壓，回升契機仍看降息預期》
月間曾因美伊爆發衝突 、雙方對和解談判的分歧及
川普開除庫克遭駁回 ，對美升息的預期及美元走強
使金價曾跌破4,000美元，但受油價已大幅回跌及疲
軟的美國就業數據使升息預期降溫 ，國際金價重新
站回4,000美元。受通膨雖降溫但仍居高不下，預估
短期仍受市場 評估升息預期變化及變現需求而震盪 ，
但本行認為下半年升息機率低 、美伊衝突帶來的不
利因素漸消除 ，回升契機將在： 1)FED轉鴿派或市
場縮減升息轉降息預期； 2)受擔憂債務可持續性 、
分散交易對手風險及多元化資產配置 ，據45%的受
訪央行仍認為將未來一年內增加黃金儲備 ，高於去

年調查，五月中國 、波蘭等央行持續購入黃金 ，年
底可望進入黃金需求旺季，維持中期上行。
